We can then evaluate the contribution of the vortex tubes to the budget of the first moments of the dissipation rate (or the enstrophy). Since the dissipation rate associated to a vortex tube can be written as
\epsilon_{\rm vt}=\nu_{\rm f}(u^{2}/\eta_{\rm K}^{2})=\langle\epsilon_{\rm f}\rangle(\lambda^{2}/\eta_{\rm K}^{2})
, we obtain that the average contribution of the dissipation rate due to vortex tubes to the budget of the mean dissipation rate is negligible since

\langle\epsilon_{\rm vt}\rangle=\langle\epsilon_{\rm f}\rangle\,\frac{\lambda^{2}}{\eta_{\rm K}^{2}}\,\frac{\eta_{\rm K}}{L}\;\Longrightarrow\;\langle\epsilon_{\rm vt}\rangle\sim\langle\epsilon_{\rm f}\rangle\left(\frac{L}{\eta_{\rm K}}\right)^{-1/3}~.

(213)

However, for the second-order moment of the dissipation rate, we get that

\langle\epsilon_{\rm vt}^{2}\rangle=\langle\epsilon_{\rm f}\rangle^{2}\,\frac{\lambda^{4}}{\eta_{\rm K}^{4}}\,\frac{\eta_{\rm K}}{L}\;\Longrightarrow\;\langle\epsilon_{\rm vt}^{2}\rangle\sim\langle\epsilon_{\rm f}\rangle^{2}\left(\frac{L}{\eta_{\rm K}}\right)^{1/3}~,

(214)

which shows that the vortex tubes provide the main contribution to the variance of the dissipation rate (similar results hold for the enstrophy). Note that we retrieve a scaling relation in line with Kolmogorov K62 hypothesis with an exponent
\mu=1/3
. To modify the crude estimation of the number of vortex tubes and its volumetric fraction to account for a Kolmogorov exponent
\mu
different from the value above (usually
\mu
is estimated at a slightly lower value than
1/3
, often
\mu\simeq 0.2-0.25
), it can be proposed that
\alpha_{\rm vt}
scales as
(\eta_{\rm K}/L)^{4/3-\mu}
, from which the same estimations yield that

\langle\epsilon_{\rm f}^{2}\rangle\sim\langle\epsilon_{\rm f}\rangle^{2}\left(L/\eta_{\rm K}\right)^{\mu}~.

(215)

Additional results of interest concern Lagrangian statistics, e.g. the acceleration of a fluid particle
a=\mathrm{d}u/\mathrm{d}t
. As already indicated, the K41 scaling for the variance of a fluid particle acceleration is
a_{\rm K}^{2}\sim\left(\langle\epsilon_{\rm f}\rangle^{3}/\nu_{\rm f}\right)^{1/2}
. When a fluid particle is caught by a vortex tube, its acceleration is
a_{\rm vt}\sim u^{2}/\eta_{\rm K}
, from which we get that
a_{\rm vt}^{2}\sim a_{\rm K}^{2}\left(\lambda\,L\right)/\eta_{\rm K}^{2}
. The second-order moment of a fluid particle acceleration deviates therefore from the K41 prediction
\langle a^{2}\rangle\sim a_{\rm K}^{2}
, since we obtain for the vortex tube contribution that

\langle a_{\rm vt}^{2}\rangle\sim a_{\rm K}^{2}\,\frac{\lambda}{\eta_{\rm K}}\sim a_{\rm K}^{2}\,Re_{\lambda}^{1/2}~,

(216)

a relation found in
Yeung and Pope 1989
but not explained by classical scaling
Yeung 2002
. Note that if we retain the more general estimate
\alpha_{\rm vt}\simeq(\eta_{\rm K}/L)^{4/3-\mu}
, the predicted scaling becomes
\langle a_{\rm vt}^{2}\rangle\sim a_{\rm K}^{2}\,Re_{\lambda}^{3\mu/2}
, with a smaller exponent, in line with more recent probes
Lanotte et al. 2013
). Drawing on these results, it is interesting to note that the effects of coherent structures are different on Eulerian and Lagrangian statistics. At a fixed location, these structures are swept by, making little marks on Eulerian statistics, whereas fluid particles can be trapped in them for longer times (of the order of
\tau_{\rm vt}
) leaving therefore potentially more significant traces on Lagrangian statistics (see an interesting account in
Lanotte et al. 2013
).

In spite of its crudely-cut features (for instance, we only considered one coherent structure and only one type of vortex tubes instead of a distribution of sizes, intensity, length, etc.), this two-fold model is useful to reveal that, not only classical signatures of intermittency are retrieved, but also that complex scaling of fluid particle statistics can be captured. This is relevant for stochastic modeling since descriptions such as the one presented above are easily implemented in the PDF framework. In practice, it means that we consider two stochastic models for the velocity of the fluid seen by discrete particles, one for the background ‘structure-less flow’ based typically on a Langevin model as introduced in preceding sections (we call this model, ‘model BG’) and one model for the flow in the near vicinity of a vortex tube typically by generating a Burgers-like flow once the vortex tube orientation and intensity are generated (we call this model, ‘model VT’). To that effect, we introduce a parent process,
S(t)
, attached to each particle, which governs the random switches from one flow region to the other: say
S(t)=1
when the particle is considered in the background flow and where the model BG is applied for
\mathbf{U}_{\rm s}
, and
S(t)=2
when the particle is captured by one vortex tube and where the model VT is applied for
\mathbf{U}_{\rm s}
. This parent process can be modeled by a generalized Poisson process jumping at random times between
S(t)=1
and
S(t)=2
at the end of duration times (i.e., the times spent in each flow type) which, according to the properties of Poisson processes, are random variables following exponential laws determined by two timescales:
\tau_{\rm BG}
the mean duration within the background flow and
\tau_{\rm vt}
the mean duration within a vortex tube. Note that
\tau_{\rm vt}
was already introduced above, while
\tau_{\rm BG}
is derived from the volumetric fraction of the vortex tubes
\alpha_{\rm vt}
as for inter-collision times in the kinetic theory (this is, thus, a foretaste of developments to come in Sec.
VI
). These conditions define the
2\times 2
transition matrix
\mathbf{M}(\Delta t)
which governs the Markov chain
S(k\Delta t)
at discrete times
t=k\Delta t
(
k\in\mathbb{N}
) with
M_{ij}(\Delta t)
the probability to have
S(t+\Delta t)=j
at time
t+\Delta t
conditioned on
S(t)=i
at time
t
:

\mathbf{M}(\Delta t)=\begin{pmatrix}e^{-\Delta t/\tau_{\rm BG}}&1-e^{-\Delta t/\tau_{\rm BG}}\\
1-e^{-\Delta t/\tau_{\rm vt}}&e^{-\Delta t/\tau_{\rm vt}}\end{pmatrix}~.

(217)

To the best of the authors’ knowledge, the development of such structure-based stochastic models is still at an early stage (though there are similarities with ideas put forward in
Kerstein 1999
), apart from one model of particle transport in wall-boundary layers for deposition problems
Guingo and Minier 2008
. In this model, a random combination of three typical structures of the near-wall region, namely sweeps, ejections and diffusion, was used to capture the particle flux towards the wall where a sticking condition was enforced for particles touching the surface. The model represents a simplified picture based on insights provided by DNS results about particle transport in wall-boundary layers, cf. Fig.
20
.

Figure 20
:
Representation of near-wall turbulent structures (left) together with side view (middle) and top view (right) of a DNS simulation showing the vorticity regions as well as the particle dynamics in the near-wall region. Reprinted with permission from
Marchioli 2017
. Copyright Springer, 2017.

The construction of this model is in line with the two-fold description introduced at the beginning of this section which is applied using a zonal decomposition: the background ‘structure-less’ flow is identified with the bulk of the flow while the three selected structures are explicitly simulated only in a thin zone in the immediate vicinity of a wall surface (say between
0\leq z^{+}\leq 100
, with
z^{+}=zu_{{\rm f},*}/\nu_{\rm f}
the wall-distance
z
normalized by the so-called wall units with
u_{{\rm f},*}
the fluid friction velocity, see accounts in
Henry et al. 2012
;
Minier 2015
). The random interplay of structures is clearly visible in the trajectories of discrete particles and satisfactory statistics for the particle deposition rate are retrieved over the whole range of particle inertia, as shown in Fig.
21
.

Figure 21
:
Results obtained with a stochastic model for near-wall turbulent structures showing the effect of the random switches between coherent structures on particle trajectories (left) and on the deposition rate (right). Reprinted with permission from
Guingo and Minier 2008
. Copyright Elsevier, 2008.

In more general terms, it is worth underlying that these structure-based models represent a new way to address complexity: instead of developing a single model which is likely to become complex itself, the leading idea is to capture complexity as the result of the random changes between the sub-models, each of which remaining simple, which are used to describe the components of an heterogeneous system. In that sense, this introduces us to similar concerns and approaches in so-called complex fluids, which are now addressed.

V
Similarities and differences with complex fluids

Though they are also referred to as soft matter, it is more appropriate for our discussion to designate by ‘complex fluids’ fluids whose microstructure involves specific components (the solute) having sizes and relaxation timescales much larger than those of the atoms/molecules of the ‘simple fluid’ (the solvent) in which they are embedded. In simple fluids, the microstructure remains regular and locally well-described by classical equilibrium statistical mechanics regardless of whether the material is flowing or at rest. At the hydrodynamical level of description, this is reflected by the linear force-flux relations entering the balance equations, such as the Newton, Fick and Fourier laws, where the only trace of molecular details is in the transport coefficients, i.e., the fluid viscosity, diffusivity and conductivity. In complex fluids, the slow and non-equilibrium response of the solute is manifested by more involved rheological laws. Typical examples include polymeric fluids, colloidal suspensions, liquid crystals, etc. (granular or glassy materials can also be considered). To overcome the difficulty of formulating the rheological properties of such complex fluids directly at the hydrodynamical or macroscopic level of description, additional structural variables are selected and a simplified (i.e., a coarse-grained) dynamical model is proposed to capture the solute response to external forces. This corresponds, for instance, to the Rouse model for polymers
Rouse Jr 1953
, Brownian dynamics for colloids
Chen and Kim 2004
or the Maier-Saupe theory for liquid crystals
Stephen and Straley 1974
.

Figure 22
:
Correspondence between objects in complex fluids and in dispersed two-phase flows.

From this brief description, it follows that complex fluids and dispersed turbulent two-phase flows have marked similarities. First, we are considering similar physical entities, with the ones handled in complex fluids appearing as smaller-size versions of the ones in dispersed two-phase flows. This correspondence is displayed in Fig.
22
, which exhibits the obvious couples: polymers and flexible fibers; colloids and discrete particles; rod-like molecules and rod-like particles. Second, there are also similarities between the main physical processes: fluctuations and dispersive effects on the one hand, and, particle-particle interactions and tendency to self-assemble on the other hand. For complex fluids, fluctuations are related to thermal noise while ‘turbulence noise’ is present in one-particle PDF descriptions of dispersed two-phase flows (note that repulsive particle-particle interactions have also a dispersive effect). The tendency to self-assemble is manifested by phase transitions in soft matter systems, e.g., solid/liquid/gas transitions in fluids, isotropic/nematic liquid crystals, paramagnetic/ferromagnetic states (if we regard them as a complex fluid), while attractive forces govern the formation of aggregates in dispersed two-phase flows.

There are, nevertheless, differences in the overall objective being pursued. Indeed, even if a specific model is applied to describe the solute dynamics, the purpose in soft matter is to close the equations governing the evolution of a complex fluid treated as a single entity. This is typically the case of polymeric fluids where the simulation of polymer motion is used to obtain the additional stress tensor, through the Kramers expression, which is added to the (Newtonian) stress tensor of the solvent. In other words, the aim is to model the complete system, solvent+solute, regarded as one equivalent (but now complex) fluid. This is not what is being done in dispersed two-phase flow modeling where the continuous (the solvent) and the dispersed (the solute) phases are treated separately as two physical systems in interaction through the exchange of mass, momentum and energy. Said otherwise, complex fluids are handled as an equivalent ‘homogeneous two-component system’ in which particle inertia is not considered significant. In effect, most, if not all, formulations of complex fluids neglect the solute inertia and rely on the Brownian limit. This raises, however, interesting issues in the proper formulation of such fast-variable techniques as well as in the resulting expression of random terms and corresponding Fokker-Planck type of closures.

In this section, we investigate the connections between complex fluids and dispersed turbulent two-phase flows by analyzing how fluctuations and random terms are accounted for. Key challenges are first brought out in Sec.
V.1
. We then concentrate on discrete particles and colloids and address the core issue of fast-variable elimination in space-dependent Langevin equations in Sec.
V.2
, before proposing some conclusions.

V.1
Neither complete order nor disorder: thermal versus turbulence noise

To bring out the differences related to how random terms are introduced in complex fluids and in turbulent dispersed two-phase flows, it is interesting to consider polymers and flexible fibers. We start with the chosen mechanical description before discussing the stochastic model applied in each case.

For polymers, it is sufficient to retain the classical Rouse model in which a polymer is described by a chain of
N_{\rm b}
beads connected by
N_{\rm b}-1
Hookean springs (see Fig.
23
)
Rouse Jr 1953
. This is a phenomenological model where the beads represent large segments of monomers so that a continuum description of the solvent is justified while the springs represent ‘entropic forces’ used to account for the elimination of many degrees of freedom from the actual polymer.

Even though a continuous representation of a flexible fiber using curvilinear coordinates (as in Slender Body Theory or in Cosserat equations
Lindner and Shelley 2015
) is possible, a typical approach consists in representing a flexible fiber as a chain of
N_{\rm rod}
connected rigid rods (see Fig.
23
). This allows to capture fiber bending and twisting motions while preserving their connectivity through constraints applied at the hinges between two adjacent rigid rods. In a way, this description amounts to a discretized version of the continuous one, each rod being a stiffened segment of the fiber. Note that a description in terms of beads and connecting FENE springs is also possible, with the rigid-segment description obtained as the limit of infinite spring stiffness
Somasi et al. 2002
;
Henry et al. 2018
. To capture the evolution of such a rod chain, we can choose to follow the center of mass of each segment, in which case each elementary mechanical object is a non-spherical rigid particle. Similarly to the polymer bead-spring description, another choice consists in following the equivalent of beads which are here the hinges around which two adjacent rods can rotate. For the sake of having two similar descriptions and without any loss of generality for the present analysis, we retain the latter mechanical representation (see Fig.
23
).

Figure 23
:
Comparison between equivalent models for flexible objects: polymer are represented by a chain of
N_{\rm b}
beads connected by Hookean springs (in complex fluids) while fibers can be simplified to
N_{\rm rod}
connected rigid rods (in dispersed two-phase flows).

In polymer theory, bead inertia is usually neglected and the Rouse model is formulated in the frame of Brownian dynamics. This means that the particle state vector attached to each polymer
\mathbf{Z}_{\rm b}
is made up by the bead positions, i.e.,
\mathbf{Z}_{\rm b}=(\mathbf{X}_{\rm b}^{[\mu]})_{\mu=1,\ldots,N_{\rm b}}
. In the absence of external forces and leaving out the interactions between polymers (dilute polymer systems), the evolution equations for
\mathbf{Z}_{\rm b}
are

\mathrm{d}\mathbf{X}_{\rm b}^{[\mu]}=\mathbf{U}_{\rm f}(t,\mathbf{X}_{\rm b}^{[\mu]}(t))\,\mathrm{d}t+\frac{\tau_{\rm p}}{m_{\rm p}}\mathbf{F}_{\rm spring}^{\rm R,[\mu]}\,\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{\frac{2k_{\rm B}\,\Theta_{\rm f}\,\tau_{\rm p}}{m_{\rm p}}}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{W}^{[\mu]}~,\;\mu=1,\ldots,N_{\rm b}~,

(218)

with
m_{\rm p}
the bead mass and where the friction coefficient is written as
m_{\rm p}/\tau_{\rm p}
. In this equation,
\mathbf{F}_{\rm spring}^{\rm R}
represent the forces acting on a bead due to the connecting springs, as expressed in the Rouse model. We introduce first a formulation in which springs can have different stiffness (i.e.,
\mathbf{k}_{\rm spring}=(k_{\rm spring}^{[\mu]})
for
\mu=1,\ldots,N_{\rm b}-1
), which for the general spring forces noted
\mathbf{F}_{\rm spring}(\mathbf{k}_{\rm spring})
gives the following expression for the force
\mathbf{F}_{\rm spring}^{[\mu]}
acting on bead
[\mu]

\mathbf{F}_{\rm spring}^{[\mu]}(\mathbf{k}_{\rm spring})=\begin{cases}\phantom{-}k_{\rm spring}^{[1]}\left(\mathbf{X}_{\rm b}^{[2]}-\mathbf{X}_{\rm b}^{[1]}\right)&\text{if}\;\mu=1,\\
\phantom{-}k_{\rm spring}^{[\mu]}\left(\mathbf{X}_{\rm b}^{[\mu+1]}-\mathbf{X}_{\rm b}^{[\mu]}\right)-k_{\rm spring}^{[\mu-1]}\left(\mathbf{X}_{\rm b}^{[\mu]}-\mathbf{X}_{\rm b}^{[\mu-1]}\right)&\text{if}\;1<\mu<N_{\rm b},\\
-k_{\rm spring}^{[N_{\rm b}-1]}\left(\mathbf{X}_{\rm b}^{[N_{\rm b}]}-\mathbf{X}_{\rm b}^{[N_{\rm b}-1]}\right)&\text{if}\;\mu=N_{\rm b}.\end{cases}

(219)

In the Rouse model, all springs have the same stiffness
k_{\rm spring}^{\rm R}
so that the vector
\mathbf{k}_{\rm spring}^{\rm R}
is a vector of identical values (i.e.,
k_{\rm spring}^{\rm R,[\mu]}=k_{\rm spring}^{\rm R},\forall\mu
), which means that in Eq. (
218
) we have
\mathbf{F}_{\rm spring}^{\rm R}=\mathbf{F}_{\rm spring}(k_{\rm spring}^{\rm R})
.

For flexible fibers, rod inertia is not necessarily neglected leading to a particle state vector with bead positions and velocities, i.e.,
\mathbf{Z}_{\rm p}=(\mathbf{X}_{\rm p}^{[\rm i]},\mathbf{U}_{\rm p}^{[\rm i]})_{{\rm i}=1,N_{\rm rod}}
. A simple dynamical model has then the form

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\mathbf{X}_{\rm p}^{[\rm i]}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{U}_{\rm p}^{[\rm i]}~,

(220a)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\mathbf{U}_{\rm p}^{[\rm i]}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\mathbf{U}_{\rm p}^{[\rm i]}-\mathbf{U}_{\rm f}(t,\mathbf{X}_{\rm p}^{[\rm i]}(t))}{\tau_{\rm p}}\,\mathrm{d}t+\frac{1}{m_{\rm p}}\mathbf{F}_{\rm spring}^{[\rm i]}(\bm{\lambda})\,\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{\frac{2k_{\rm B}\,\Theta_{\rm f}}{m_{\rm p}\,\tau_{\rm p}}}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{W}^{[\rm i]}~,

(220b)

where the vector
\bm{\lambda}/m_{\rm p}
can be thought of as a set of spring stiffness. When the flexible fiber is made up by connected rigid rods,
\bm{\lambda}
represents the Lagrange multipliers used to enforce the non-extensibility of each rod, obtained from the constraint that
|\mathbf{X}_{\rm p}^{[\rm i+1]}-\mathbf{X}_{\rm p}^{[\rm i]}|
remains constant (for
i=1,\ldots,N_{\rm rod}-1
). Note that Brownian effects are retained in Eqs. (
220
) but are expressed by a white-noise term appearing in the velocity equation, cf. Eq. (
220b
), rather than directly in the position equation, cf. (
220
), as in Brownian dynamics (see further discussion in Sec.
V.2
).

Whatever the selected way to account for thermal noise, it is seen from Eq. (
218
) and Eq. (
220b
) that its introduction is straightforward and consists in an isotropic diffusion term in front of a Wiener process. A crucial point is that, at the bead or particle level of description, thermal noise is independent between distinct locations. This means that the formulation given, for instance, in Eq. (
218
) remains exact regardless of the fact that we track one or a set of
N
particles.

Far different is the situation with respect to turbulent flows in the case of non-fully resolved velocity fields. Note that this situation does not arise for polymers transported by laminar flows (most of the time, even simple laminar shear flows), where the velocities at the bead locations,
(\mathbf{U}_{\rm f}(t,\mathbf{X}_{\rm b}^{[\mu]}(t)))_{\mu=1,N_{\rm b}}
in Eq. (
218
), are known. The same remains valid for the velocity equation of each segment or bead of the flexible fiber, cf. Eq. (
220b
), but only provided that we are dealing with fully-resolved turbulent flows by which we have access to the values of the instantaneous velocities at the bead locations,
(\mathbf{U}_{\rm f}(t,\mathbf{X}_{\rm p}^{[\rm i]}(t)))_{i=1,N_{\rm rod}}
. In the case of high Reynolds-number turbulent flows or when we have only access to a reduced statistical description of the velocity field, we are then faced with the difficulty of having to reconstruct the set of the
N_{\rm rod}
fluid velocities seen,
(\mathbf{U}^{[\rm i]}_{\rm s}(t)=\mathbf{U}_{\rm f}(t,\mathbf{X}_{\rm p}^{[\rm i]}(t)))_{i=1,N_{\rm rod}}
. In that case, the Langevin model studied in preceding sections cannot be applied since it corresponds to a one-particle PDF model and, therefore, to a one-point type of closure. As already emphasized, there is no spatial information in present one-particle model for the velocity of the fluid seen. It is thus clear that a set of Langevin models for
(\mathbf{U}^{[\rm i]}_{\rm s}(t))_{i=1,N_{\rm rod}}
, with a drift vector
\mathbf{A}^{[\rm i]}_{\rm s}
and diffusion matrix
\mathbf{B}^{[\rm i]}_{\rm s}
(which need not be precised for the present discussion), such as

\mathrm{d}\mathbf{U}^{[\rm i]}_{\rm s}(t)=\mathbf{A}^{[\rm i]}_{\rm s}\,\mathrm{d}t+\mathbf{B}^{[\rm i]}_{\rm s}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{W}^{[\rm i]}~,

(221)

but with independent Wiener process,
(\mathbf{W}^{[\rm i]}(t))_{i=1,N_{\rm rod}}
, between each rod segment or bead
[\rm i]
would grossly misrepresent the spatial correlations which are one of the hallmarks of turbulent flows. In other words, we are dealing with ‘turbulence noise’ which is neither order nor complete disorder. For general non-homogeneous turbulent flows where velocities can deviate from Gaussianity, generating a set of
N
fluid velocities seen,
(\mathbf{U}^{[\rm i]}_{\rm s}(t))_{i=1,N}
, that respect known correlations in time (this is already achieved) but also in space (this is an open issue) appears as a quite challenging task.

Comparing polymers in laminar flows and flexible fibers in non-fully resolved turbulent flows is relevant to illustrate that modeling ‘turbulence noise’ is more complicated than adding a
k_{\rm B}\,\Theta_{\rm f}
diffusion term to a deterministic mechanical model. In the present review where we concentrate on small discrete particles whose counterpart among soft matter systems is colloidal suspensions, the above issue is less sensitive. We can then ask: is the description of colloidal suspensions, in particular Brownian motion and the diffusive limit (Brownian dynamics), contained in dispersed two-phase flow models? To provide answers to this question, we consider first how Brownian motion is accounted for and, then, discuss recent results on the overdamped limit of Langevin equations. These results are of interest for non-equilibrium dynamics of soft matter.

V.2
Fast-variable elimination and the Brownian limit

V.2.1
Accounting for Brownian motion

The introduction of Brownian effects in particle stochastic models is fairly well-established and goes back to the contributions of
Einstein et al. 1905
and
Langevin 1908
. We benefit now from the rigorous framework of Ito calculus for stochastic differential equations (see Sec.
II.3
) and it is therefore interesting to revisit its main points since they are useful to introduce fast-variable elimination. To account for Brownian motion, it is best to consider first Brownian particles in a fluid at rest. Following Langevin’s original point of view
Langevin 1908
, the classical formulation consists in writing the particle velocity equation as the sum of a linear return-to-equilibrium term due to fluid friction and a random one written in terms of the increments of a Wiener process. In a one-dimensional notation, this gives

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}X_{\rm p}

\displaystyle=U_{\rm p}\,\mathrm{d}t~,

(222a)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}U_{\rm p}

\displaystyle=-\frac{U_{\rm p}}{\tau_{\rm p}}\,\mathrm{d}t+K\,\mathrm{d}W~,

(222b)

where
K
is a diffusion coefficient to be determined. From classical statistical mechanics and the equipartition of energy, we know that
1/2\,m_{\rm p}\langle(U_{\rm p})^{2}\rangle
reaches a constant value in the long-time limit which is equal to
1/2\,k_{\rm B}\,\Theta_{\rm f}
. Then, an immediate application of Ito calculus yields that

\frac{\mathrm{d}\langle(U_{\rm p})^{2}\rangle}{\mathrm{d}t}=0\quad\Longrightarrow\quad K^{2}=\frac{2\langle(U_{\rm p})^{2}\rangle}{\tau_{\rm p}}=\frac{2k_{\rm B}\,\Theta_{\rm f}}{m_{\rm p}\,\tau_{\rm p}}~,

(223)

which is the form already given in Eq. (
220b
). Note that this a straightforward expression of what is referred to as a fluctuation-dissipation theorem, as already noted in Sec.
II.3
.

When
\tau_{\rm p}
and
K
are constants,
U_{\rm p}
is a simple OU process and the exact behavior of the position of Brownian particles can be worked out before taking the limit
\tau_{\rm p}\to 0
(for example by substituting
r
with
X_{\rm p}
,
T_{\rm L}
with
\tau_{\rm p}
, and
\sqrt{C_{0}\,\langle\epsilon_{\rm f}\rangle}
with
K
in Eq. (
190
)). There is, however, a short-cut method which is often used and which consists in neglecting the particle acceleration in the limit of small particle inertia (manifested here by small values of the relaxation timescale
\tau_{\rm p}
). For example, if we consider a more general evolution equation with the fluid velocity seen in the drag force when the fluid is not at rest and a force
F_{\rm p}
due to external actions or to other particles,

\mathrm{d}U_{\rm p}=\frac{U_{\rm s}-U_{\rm p}}{\tau_{\rm p}}\,\mathrm{d}t+F_{\rm p}\,\mathrm{d}t+K\,\mathrm{d}W~,

(224)

we get from enforcing
\mathrm{d}U_{\rm p}\simeq 0
that the particle position equation
\mathrm{d}X_{\rm p}=U_{\rm p}\,\mathrm{d}t
becomes

\mathrm{d}X_{\rm p}=U_{\rm s}\,\mathrm{d}t+\tau_{\rm p}F_{\rm p}\,\mathrm{d}t+\left(\tau_{\rm p}K\right)\mathrm{d}W~.

(225)

This corresponds to the purely diffusive behavior of Brownian particles, from which we retrieve the classical Einstein relation for the position diffusion coefficient since

\mathcal{D}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\tau_{\rm p}K\right)^{2}=\frac{k_{\rm B}\,\Theta_{\rm f}}{3\pi d_{\rm p}\rho_{\rm f}\nu_{\rm f}}~,

(226)

and the classical formulation for the Brownian particle position equation

\mathrm{d}X_{\rm p}=U_{\rm s}\,\mathrm{d}t+\tau_{\rm p}F_{\rm p}\,\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{2\mathcal{D}}\,\mathrm{d}W~.

(227)

This is the basis of the slaving principle (introduced in Sec.
III.2
), obtained by this heuristic formulation of fast-variable elimination techniques (see also Fig.
24
). When the degrees of freedom are separated into slow and fast variables (here, the particle position and velocity, respectively), and even when the drift and diffusion coefficients are functions of the slow variables, the same reasoning is assumed to remain valid leading to the same diffusive limit for the slow variable. In the present case, this still gives Eq. (
225
) with
\tau_{\rm p}=\tau_{\rm p}(t,X_{\rm p})
and
K=K(t,X_{\rm p})
. Yet, though intuitive, this short-cut method of the slaving principle lacks rigor and raises two concerns.

Figure 24
:
Representation of the key issue encountered when formulating the over-damped Langevin.

The first one is the very loose manner with which the limit
\tau_{\rm p}\to 0
is taken and the proper sense with which terms such as
(\tau_{\rm p}K)
and
\tau_{\rm p}F_{\rm p}
are to be regarded in that limit. To get around this difficulty, we introduce a small parameter
\chi
and consider the following stochastic model

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}X_{\rm p}^{(\chi)}

\displaystyle=U_{\rm p}^{(\chi)}\,\mathrm{d}t~,

(228a)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}U_{\rm p}^{(\chi)}

\displaystyle=\frac{U_{\rm s}-U_{\rm p}^{(\chi)}}{\chi\,\tau_{\rm p}}\,\mathrm{d}t+\frac{1}{\chi}F_{\rm p}\,\mathrm{d}t+\frac{K}{\chi}\,\mathrm{d}W~,

(228b)

where the timescale varies as
\chi\,\tau_{\rm p}
while the force and the diffusion coefficient scale as
F_{\rm p}/\chi
and
K/\chi
, respectively. This allows to formalize the previous manipulations and give sense to the limit
\tau_{\rm p}\to 0
and
K\to+\infty
with
\tau_{\rm p}\,K
remaining constant. Other parametrizations have been proposed but have been shown to be equivalent or even of a less general application for time-dependent force, i.e. when
F_{\rm p}=F_{\rm p}(t,X_{\rm p})
(see a comprehensive discussion of this point in
(
Minier 2016
, section 9.3)
). The short-cut method of fast-variable elimination takes now a more satisfactory expression since we have

U_{\rm p}^{(\chi)}\mathrm{d}t\simeq U_{\rm s}\,\mathrm{d}t+\tau_{\rm p}F_{\rm p}\,\mathrm{d}t+\left(K\tau_{\rm p}\right)\mathrm{d}W~,

(229)

leading, in a more rigorous manner, to the diffusive limit of
\mathrm{d}X_{\rm p}^{(\chi)}
when
\chi\to 0
, which is Eq. (
225
). Note that the particle mean kinetic energy
\langle(U_{\rm p}^{(\chi)})^{2}\rangle
scales now as
1/2K^{2}/(\chi\,\tau_{\rm p})
, cf. Eq. (
223
), and we retrieve that it is the diffusive coefficient,
\langle(U_{\rm p}^{(\chi)})^{2}\rangle\times(\chi\,\tau_{\rm p})
that tends to a finite limit, which is, of course, the Einstein diffusion coefficient.

The second concern is about the validity of the assumption that the diffusive limit can be derived simply by neglecting the particle acceleration, i.e. by enforcing directly
\mathrm{d}U_{\rm p}^{(\chi)}=0
. In particular, does this short-cut method remain valid when the coefficients are not constant but are functions of the particle position and, therefore, of the slow variable itself? To answer this question, we consider now the diffusive limit of ‘turbulence noise’ for fluid particles (the tracer-particle limit without Brownian motion) where the timescale and the diffusion coefficient in general Langevin models are indeed space dependent.

V.2.2
Brownian dynamics in turbulent flows

In
(
Minier 2016
, section 9.3)
, it is shown that the most general way to derive the limit of over-damped Langevin equations is to consider for
\mathbf{Z}=(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{U})
the system

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}X_{i}

\displaystyle=U_{i}\,\mathrm{d}t~,

(230a)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}U_{i}

\displaystyle=G_{ij}(t,\mathbf{X}(t))\left[U_{j}-\Phi_{j}(t,\mathbf{X}(t))\right]\,\mathrm{d}t+\sigma_{ij}(t,\mathbf{X}(t))\,\mathrm{d}W_{j}~,

(230b)

and to study the limit when
\chi\to 0
of a parametrized version where a small parameter
\chi
is introduced

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}X^{(\chi)}_{i}

\displaystyle=U^{(\chi)}_{i}\,\mathrm{d}t~,

(231a)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}U^{(\chi)}_{i}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\chi}G_{ij}(t,\mathbf{X}^{(\chi)}(t))\left[U^{(\chi)}_{j}-\Phi_{j}(t,\mathbf{X}^{(\chi)}(t))\right]\,\mathrm{d}t+\frac{1}{\chi}\sigma_{ij}(t,\mathbf{X}^{(\chi)}(t))\,\mathrm{d}W_{j}~.

(231b)

As indicated for Brownian particles, the introduction of the parameter
\chi
formalizes the limit we wish to take (in a scalar version, we consider
G^{-1}\to 0
and
\sigma\to+\infty
so that
G^{-1}\sigma
remains constant). This is done by multiplying directly the drift and diffusion coefficients appearing in Eq. (
230
) by
1/\chi
and the same notations are kept for these coefficients in Eq. (
231b
). In a physics-oriented analysis, it is best to identify first a small parameter and describe then how the drift and diffusion coefficients scale with it, as shown below for the case of fluid particles in turbulent flows. However, we are first concerned with the correct mathematical limit of Eqs. (
231
), with
\mathbf{G}
and
\bm{\sigma}
now regular functions. In Eqs. (
231
), where the specific dependencies of the drift and diffusion coefficients were kept for clarity but are left out from now on for the sake of simplicity, the matrix
\mathbf{G}
is assumed to be invertible and
\bm{\Phi}(t,\mathbf{x})
is a known field that remains regular.

It is then demonstrated that, in the limit
\chi\to 0
, the over-damped Langevin limit model is a stochastic diffusion process for particle positions which writes

\mathrm{d}X_{i}=\Phi_{i}\,\mathrm{d}t-\frac{\partial G^{-1}_{ij}}{\partial x_{k}}A_{kj}\mathrm{d}t+\left(G^{-1}\sigma\right)_{ij}\mathrm{d}W_{j}~,

(232)

where the matrix
\mathbf{A}
stands for
A_{ij}=\lim_{\chi\to 0}\left(\chi\langle U^{(\chi)}_{i}U^{(\chi)}_{j}\rangle\right)
and is the solution of the matrix equation

\mathbf{G}\mathbf{A}+\mathbf{A}\mathbf{G}^{\bot}=-\bm{\sigma}\bm{\sigma}^{\bot}~.

(233)

To apply these results to the case of fluid particles in turbulent flows, for which we use the notations
\mathbf{Z}_{\rm f}=(\mathbf{X}_{\rm f},\mathbf{U}_{\rm f})
, the relevant choice for the field
\Phi(t,\mathbf{x})
is to take

\phi_{i}=\langle U_{{\rm f},i}\rangle+G_{ij}^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{\rho_{\rm f}}\frac{\partial\langle P_{\rm f}\rangle}{\partial x_{j}}\right)~,

(234)

which means that both
\langle\mathbf{U}_{\rm f}\rangle
and
\langle P_{\rm f}\rangle
must remain regular whereas the finite nature of the matrix
\mathbf{A}
shows that the kinetic energy grows unbounded as
1/\chi
(this is in line with the remark above concerning Brownian motion). This corresponds to a situation where the mean velocity field remains regular but where the second-order moments of the fluctuating velocity components grow as
1/\chi
, which implies that
\mathbf{A}
is also given by
A_{ij}=\lim_{\chi\to 0}\left(\chi\langle u^{(\chi)}_{i}u^{(\chi)}_{j}\rangle\right)
. This explains the introduction of the mean-pressure gradient in
\bm{\phi}
which, according to Eq. (
231b
), scales as
1/\chi
and compensates the divergence of the Reynolds stresses in the mean Navier-Stokes equations to ensure that the mean velocity field remains regular. This important point is detailed in
(
Minier 2016
, section 9.3)
and is also revisited below. With these conditions, the proper over-damped Langevin limit model for fluid particles is

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}X_{{\rm f},i}

\displaystyle=\langle U_{{\rm f},i}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+G_{ij}^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{\rho_{\rm f}}\frac{\partial\langle P_{\rm f}\rangle}{\partial x_{j}}\right)\,\mathrm{d}t-\frac{\partial G^{-1}_{ij}}{\partial x_{k}}A_{kj}\mathrm{d}t+\left(G^{-1}\sigma\right)_{ij}\mathrm{d}W_{j}~,

(235)

\displaystyle=\langle U_{{\rm f},i}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+G_{ij}^{-1}\left\{\frac{1}{\rho_{\rm f}}\frac{\partial\langle P_{\rm f}\rangle}{\partial x_{j}}+\frac{\partial A_{jk}}{\partial x_{k}}\right\}\mathrm{d}t-\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{k}}\left[G^{-1}_{ij}A_{jk}\right]\mathrm{d}t+\left(G^{-1}\sigma\right)_{ij}\mathrm{d}W_{j}~,

(236)

\displaystyle=\langle U_{{\rm f},i}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t-\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{k}}\left[G^{-1}_{ij}A_{jk}\right]\mathrm{d}t+\left(G^{-1}\sigma\right)_{ij}\mathrm{d}W_{j}~,

(237)

since the term between the brackets on the rhs of Eq. (
236
) vanishes due to the enforced condition of the mean pressure gradient. In the corresponding Fokker-Planck equation, the diffusion matrix,
\mathbf{D}
, is then

\mathbf{D}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathbf{G}^{-1}\bm{\sigma}\right)\left(\mathbf{G}^{-1}\bm{\sigma}\right)^{\bot}~.

(238)

With
\mathbf{B}
a matrix such that
\mathbf{B}\mathbf{B}^{\bot}=2\mathbf{D}
, we obtain after some tedious but straightforward manipulations the final resulting form of the diffusion equation for fluid particle positions in the over-damped limit as

\mathrm{d}X_{{\rm f},i}=\langle U_{{\rm f},i}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+\frac{\partial D_{ik}}{\partial x_{k}}\,\mathrm{d}t+B_{ik}\,\mathrm{d}W_{k}.

(239)

In the case where
\mathbf{G}
and
\bm{\sigma}
are isotropic tensors,
G_{ij}=-1/T_{\rm L}\delta_{ij}
and
\sigma_{ij}=\sigma\delta_{ij}
, we have the simple form

\mathrm{d}X_{{\rm f},i}=\langle U_{{\rm f},i}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}}\left[\frac{1}{2}\left(T_{\rm L}\,\sigma\right)^{2}\right]\,\mathrm{d}t+\left(T_{\rm L}\,\sigma\right)\,\mathrm{d}W_{i}.

(240)

These results call for some comments which, for the sake of simplicity, are based on the case of a scalar diffusivity to avoid more cumbersome tensorial notations.

(1)

The derivation of the diffusive limit shows that the elimination of the fast-variable
\mathbf{U}_{\rm f}
leads to both a diffusive and a drift term in the particle position equation

\mathrm{d}X_{{\rm f},i}=\langle U_{{\rm f},i}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+\frac{\partial\Gamma_{\rm ft}}{\partial x_{i}}+\sqrt{2\Gamma_{\rm ft}}\,\mathrm{d}W_{i}~.

(241)

where we have used
\Gamma_{\rm ft}=\left(T_{\rm L}\,\sigma\right)^{2}/2
. This is to be compared to the naive formulation which gives

\mathrm{d}X^{(\rm naive)}_{{\rm f},i}=\langle U_{{\rm f},i}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{2\Gamma_{\rm ft}}\,\mathrm{d}W_{i}~,

(242)

where the drift term
\partial\Gamma_{\rm ft}/\partial x_{i}
is missing.

(2)

When we consider a conserved scalar attached to each particle, i.e.
\mathrm{d}\phi_{\rm f}=0
, then
\langle\phi_{\rm f}\rangle
represents the mean particle concentration. In sample space, the Fokker-Planck equation for
p(t;\mathbf{y},\psi_{\rm f})
is

\frac{\partial p}{\partial t}=-\frac{\partial\left[\,\langle U_{{\rm f},i}\rangle\,p\,\right]}{\partial y_{i}}-\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{i}}\left[\left(\frac{\partial\Gamma_{\rm ft}}{\partial x_{i}}\right)\,p\,\right]+\frac{\partial^{2}\left[\,\Gamma_{\rm ft}\,p\,\right]}{\partial y_{i}\partial y_{i}}~,

(243)

where the diffusion matrix involves only diagonal (even isotropic) terms since we are dealing with a scalar diffusivity (the general case with a full second-order diffusivity tensor is addressed below). By integration over the sample space variable
\psi_{\rm f}
corresponding to
\phi_{\rm f}
, we see that the mean concentration
\langle c_{\rm f}\rangle(t,\mathbf{x})
satisfies the same equation in physical space

\frac{\partial\langle c_{\rm f}\rangle}{\partial t}=-\frac{\partial\left[\,\langle U_{{\rm f},i}\rangle\,\langle c_{\rm f}\rangle\,\right]}{\partial x_{i}}-\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}}\left[\left(\frac{\partial\,\Gamma_{\rm ft}}{\partial x_{i}}\right)\,\langle c_{\rm f}\rangle\,\right]+\frac{\partial^{2}\left[\,\Gamma_{\rm ft}\,\langle c_{\rm f}\rangle\,\right]}{\partial x_{i}\partial x_{i}}~.

(244)

Both forms show that an uniform particle concentration field is conserved, as it should be for incompressible flows (since
\partial\langle U_{{\rm f},i}\rangle/\partial x_{i}=0
). In contrast, the naive diffusive limit, expressed by Eq. (
242
), would produce a spurious drift term due to a non-compensated variable diffusion coefficient
\Gamma_{\rm ft}(\mathbf{x})
. Since each particle represents the same amount of mass, we would then induce artificial accumulations/depletions of mass at variance with the basic property of incompressible flows.

(3)

By considering the position pdf
p(t;\mathbf{y})
, obtained as the marginal of the position-scalar one
p(t;\mathbf{y},\psi_{\rm f})
, and rearranging the last two terms on the rhs of Eq. (
243
), we obtain

\frac{\partial p}{\partial t}=-\frac{\partial\left[\,\langle U_{{\rm f},i}\rangle\,p\,\right]}{\partial y_{i}}+\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{i}}\left[\Gamma_{\rm ft}\frac{\partial p}{\partial y_{i}}\right]~,

(245)

and by further combinations

\frac{\partial p}{\partial t}=-\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{i}}\left[\,\left(\langle U_{{\rm f},i}\rangle-\Gamma_{\rm ft}\frac{\partial\ln(p)}{\partial y_{i}}\right)\,p\,\right]~.

(246)

As such, the random term due to the elimination of the fast-variable
\mathbf{U}_{\rm f}
appears as an equivalent drift term in the equation for the slow-variable
\mathbf{X}_{\rm f}
. If we remember that such drift terms are obtained as
(T_{\rm L}\,F_{\rm rand})
for the case of an applied force
F_{\rm rand}
in the particle velocity equation, cf. Eqs. (
224
) and (
225
) substituting
T_{\rm L}
with
\tau_{\rm p}
and
F_{\rm rand}
with
F_{\rm p}
, this formulation presents random effects as being due to an equivalent ‘entropic force’ which, per unit mass, is

F_{\rm rand}=\frac{\Gamma_{\rm ft}}{T_{\rm L}}\,\frac{\partial\ln(p)}{\partial x_{i}}\quad\text{or}\quad F_{\rm rand}=\frac{\Gamma_{\rm ft}}{T_{\rm L}}\,\frac{\partial\ln(\langle c_{\rm f}\rangle)}{\partial x_{i}}~,

(247)

where the second expression makes use of the proportionality between
p(t,\mathbf{x})
and
\langle c_{\rm f}\rangle(t,\mathbf{x})
. Nevertheless, this correspondence should be taken with some care as the two formulations do not have the same mathematical support. Indeed, the first formulation, in Eqs. (
241
) or (
243
), corresponds to a well-established SDE in which the drift and diffusion coefficients are functions of the stochastic process
\mathbf{X}_{\rm f}
or, perhaps, of statistics derived from the PDF in the extended sense referred to as McKean SDEs (see an interesting discussion, in physical terms, of the differences in
(
Öttinger 1996
, section 3.3.4)
and the related important issue of propagation of chaos in processes with mean-field interactions). However, the second formulation, given by
F_{\rm rand}
on the left side of Eq. (
247
), is written in terms of the PDF itself and does not fall into the category of mathematically well-defined drift or diffusion terms in SDEs.

(4)

To underline the previous point, we can revert to Brownian particles and substitute
\tau_{\rm p}
with
T_{\rm L}
, knowing that
\left(k_{\rm B}\,\Theta_{\rm f}\right)/m_{\rm p}
is the particle velocity variance. We retrieve the Einstein formula for the diffusivity since
\Gamma_{\rm B}=\langle U_{\rm p}^{2}\rangle\,\tau_{\rm p}=\left(k_{\rm B}\,\Theta_{\rm f}\,\tau_{\rm p}\right)/m_{\rm p}
(with
m_{\rm p}/\tau_{\rm p}
the friction coefficient). This leads to the position-pdf equation for Brownian particles

\frac{\partial p}{\partial t}=-\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{i}}\left[\,\left(\langle U_{{\rm f},i}\rangle-\frac{k_{\rm B}\,\Theta_{\rm f}\,\tau_{\rm p}}{m_{\rm p}}\,\frac{\partial\ln(p)}{\partial y_{i}}\right)\,p\,\right]~,

(248)

involving what is referred to in polymer physics as a ‘smoothed Brownian force’,
\mathbf{F}_{\rm B}=k_{\rm B}\,\Theta_{\rm f}\,\bm{\nabla}\ln(p)
(see discussions in
Öttinger 1996
;
Doi and Edwards 1988
). The direct introduction of such ‘smoothed Brownian forces’ appears, however, difficult to grasp at first sight and it is believed that the present approach offers more justification. Indeed, compared to the ‘raw Brownian term’ which is the diffusive coefficient involving the Wiener process
\sqrt{2\Gamma_{\rm ft}}\,\mathrm{d}W
, the smoothed version arises from the existence of a corresponding non-zero drift term which is
\partial\Gamma_{\rm ft}/\partial x_{i}
(or
\partial\Gamma_{\rm B}/\partial x_{i}
): it is a combination of ‘complete disorder’ (diffusion) and ‘order’ (drift) that leads to these smoothed forces.

(5)

It is worth noting that the diffusive limit of overdamped Langevin models has been obtained here by following the trajectory point of view and using Ito stochastic calculus. In other words, we have not relied on the projection-operator formalism, which is often believed to be needed (see the discussion in
(
Gardiner 2009
, chapter 8)
) but which, although relevant, appears as not mandatory.

Yet, these results are in line with the way fluctuations are proposed to be added in the GENERIC framework (see
(
Öttinger 2005
, section 1.2.5)
) and with subsequent derivations based on the (rather formal) projection-operator techniques (see
(
Öttinger 2005
, section 6.3.3)
). The present approach works the other way around and obtains the Fokker-Planck equation for the slow variables after having derived their SDEs. In physical terms, present ideas are perhaps best cast with the general terminology of force-flux relations, as in thermodynamics. To illustrate that point, let us consider again the simple toy model used in Sec.
III.2
with a slow variable
X_{\rm slow}
whose time-rate-of-change is a fast-variable
X_{\rm fast}
, i.e.
\mathrm{d}X_{\rm slow}/\mathrm{d}t=X_{\rm fast}
. The exact but unclosed equation for the marginal pdf
p(t;y)
is

\frac{\partial\,p(t;y)}{\partial t}=-\frac{\partial}{\partial y}\left[\,\langle\,X_{\rm fast}\,|_{X_{\rm slow}=y}\rangle\,p(t;y)\,\right]~.

(249)

When
X_{\rm fast}
becomes a fast variable in the sense studied above, that is with
\langle X_{\rm fast}^{2}\rangle\,T_{X_{\rm fast}}
tending towards a finite coefficient
\Gamma_{X_{\rm fast}}(x)
which can depend on the local value of the slow variable
X_{\rm slow}
, we have

\langle\,X_{\rm fast}\,|_{X_{\rm slow}=y}\rangle\,p(t;y)=-\Gamma_{\rm X_{\rm fast}}(y)\frac{\partial\,p(t;y)}{\partial y}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\frac{\partial\,p(t;y)}{\partial t}=\frac{\partial}{\partial y}\left[\Gamma_{\rm X_{\rm fast}}(y)\frac{\partial\,p(t;y)}{\partial y}\right]~,

(250)

with the same form of the Fokker-Planck equation. Therefore, the ‘flux’
\langle\,X_{\rm fast}\,|_{X=y}\rangle
is

\langle\,X_{\rm fast}\,|_{X_{\rm slow}=y}\rangle=-\Gamma_{\rm X_{\rm fast}}(y)\frac{\partial\ln\left[\,p(t;y)\,\right]}{\partial y}~,

(251)

and is proportional to the gradient of the ‘entropic force’ , since the statistical entropy is
S\simeq\ln(p)
.

V.2.3
Non-local and local closures

There is an interesting connection between the introduction of white-noise effects in the evolution equations of the selected variables and local/non-local closures in the transport equations satisfied by moments of these variables. To exemplify that connection, we consider first the example of a conserved scalar
\phi_{\rm f}
and two sets of fluid-particle-attached variables. When the particle velocity is retained in the particle state vector, which becomes
\mathbf{Z}_{\rm f}=(\mathbf{X}_{\rm f},\mathbf{U}_{\rm f},\phi_{\rm f})
, and is modeled, for instance, by a Langevin model, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}X_{{\rm f},i}

\displaystyle=U_{{\rm f},i}\,\mathrm{d}t~,

(252a)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}U_{{\rm f},i}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\rho_{\rm f}}\frac{\partial\langle P_{\rm f}\rangle}{\partial x_{i}}\,\mathrm{d}t+G_{ij}\left(U_{{\rm f},j}-\langle U_{{\rm f},j}\rangle\right)\,\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{C_{0}\,\langle\epsilon_{\rm f}\rangle}\,\mathrm{d}W_{i}~,

(252b)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\phi_{\rm f}

\displaystyle=0~.

(252c)

In that case, the fluid-particle acceleration is regarded as a fast variable and is eliminated, leaving the modeled Langevin terms in Eq. (
252b
). We are then handling a velocity-scalar PDF,
p(t;\mathbf{x},\mathbf{V}_{\rm f},\psi_{\rm f})
, which follows a Fokker-Planck equation. From that equation, it is easy to derive the equation satisfied by the mean scalar field
\langle\phi_{\rm f}\rangle(t,\mathbf{x})
, which is

\frac{\partial\langle\phi_{\rm f}\rangle}{\partial t}+\langle U_{{\rm f},i}\rangle\frac{\partial\langle\phi_{\rm f}\rangle}{\partial x_{i}}=-\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}}\left[\langle u_{{\rm f},i}\phi^{\prime}_{\rm f}\rangle\right]~,

(253)

where
\phi^{\prime}_{\rm f}=\phi_{\rm f}-\langle\phi_{\rm f}\rangle
is the scalar fluctuation and
u_{{\rm f},i}
is the velocity fluctuation. In this equation,
\langle u_{{\rm f},i}\phi^{\prime}_{\rm f}\rangle
stands for the scalar flux and is obtained as the solution of the equation

\frac{\partial\langle u_{{\rm f},i}\phi^{\prime}_{\rm f}\rangle}{\partial t}+\frac{\partial\langle u_{{\rm f},k}u_{{\rm f},i}\phi^{\prime}_{\rm f}\rangle}{\partial x_{k}}=-\langle u_{{\rm f},k}\phi^{\prime}_{\rm f}\rangle\frac{\partial\langle U_{{\rm f},i}\rangle}{\partial x_{k}}-\langle u_{{\rm f},i}u_{{\rm f},k}\rangle\frac{\partial\langle\phi_{\rm f}\rangle}{\partial x_{k}}+G_{ij}\langle u_{{\rm f},j}\phi^{\prime}_{\rm f}\rangle~,

(254)

which is also extracted from the Fokker-Planck equation. The important point is that the scalar flux
\langle u_{{\rm f},i}\phi^{\prime}_{\rm f}\rangle
is a non-local quantity in physical space since it results from a transport equation.

When the particle velocity is regarded as a fast-variable and eliminated with the techniques outlined above, the particle state vector is reduced to
\mathbf{Z}_{\rm f}=(\mathbf{X}_{\rm f},\phi)
and the evolution equations have the form

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}X_{{\rm f},i}

\displaystyle=\langle U_{{\rm f},i}\rangle\mathrm{d}t+\frac{\partial\Gamma_{{\rm ft},ik}}{\partial x_{k}}\mathrm{d}t+B_{{\rm ft},ik}\,\mathrm{d}W_{k}~,

(255a)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\phi_{\rm f}

\displaystyle=0~.

(255b)

In Eq. (
255a
), the diffusive matrix
\mathbf{B}_{\rm ft}
corresponds to
(2\bm{\Gamma}_{\rm ft})^{1/2}
, which means that
\mathbf{B}_{\rm ft}\mathbf{B}^{\bot}_{\rm ft}=2\bm{\Gamma}_{\rm ft}
, while the diffusivity tensor is
\bm{\Gamma}_{\rm ft}=1/2\mathbf{G}^{-1}\bm{\sigma}\bm{\sigma}^{\bot}(\mathbf{G}^{\bot})^{-1}
with
\bm{\sigma}=\sqrt{C_{0}\langle\epsilon_{\rm f}\rangle}\,\bm{\delta}
(note that this is the same expression as the general diffusivity tensor given above, cf. Eq. (
238
), but we use
\bm{\Gamma}_{\rm ft}
for this specific choice of
\bm{\sigma}
). The Fokker-Planck equation for the PDF
p(t;y_{\rm f},\psi_{\rm f})
is then

\frac{\partial p}{\partial t}=-\frac{\partial\left[\,\langle U_{{\rm f},i}\rangle\,p\,\right]}{\partial y_{i}}+\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{i}}\left[\Gamma_{{\rm ft},ij}\frac{\partial p}{\partial y_{j}}\right]~.

(256)

Note that Eqs. (
241
) and (
243
) or Eq. (
245
) correspond to the case where
\mathbf{G}
and
\bm{\sigma}
are isotropic tensors. From Eq. (
256
), it follows that the mean scalar field
\langle\phi_{\rm f}\rangle(t,\mathbf{x})
evolves now as

\frac{\partial\langle\phi_{\rm f}\rangle}{\partial t}+\langle U_{{\rm f},i}\rangle\frac{\partial\langle\phi_{\rm f}\rangle}{\partial x_{i}}=\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}}\left[\Gamma_{{\rm ft},ij}\left(\frac{\partial\langle\phi_{\rm f}\rangle}{\partial x_{j}}\right)\right]~.

(257)

The important point now is that the scalar flux
\langle u_{{\rm f},i}\phi^{\prime}_{\rm f}\rangle
is no longer the solution of a transport equation, as in Eq. (
254
), but is expressed locally in physical space through an eddy-diffusivity relation

\langle u_{{\rm f},i}\phi^{\prime}_{\rm f}\rangle=-\Gamma_{{\rm ft},ij}\left(\frac{\partial\langle\phi_{\rm f}\rangle}{\partial x_{j}}\right)~.

(258)

We have moved from a non-local closure when the particle velocity is explicitly modeled to a local closure when it is eliminated (as displayed in Fig.
25
). As such, this seems to reflect essentially the selection of the variables retained in the particle state vector. However, since this selection follows the idea of decomposing between slow and fast variables and that the trace of fast variables is manifested by white-noise terms (thus by increments of Wiener processes in the SDEs), the connection is actually with white-noise effects.

Figure 25
:
Representation of non-local versus local closures from a Langevin model for fluid particles.

A similar analysis can be carried out for the mean velocity field but requires careful bookkeeping of the unbounded terms that cancel each other out. To that effect, it proves useful to follow up the ideas introduced in
(
Minier 2016
, section 9.3.3)
and consider for
\mathbf{Z}_{\rm f}=(\mathbf{X}_{\rm f},\mathbf{U}_{\rm f})
the stochastic system written as

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}X^{(\chi)}_{{\rm f},i}

\displaystyle=U^{(\chi)}_{{\rm f},i}\,\mathrm{d}t~,

(259a)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}U^{(\chi)}_{{\rm f},i}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\rho_{\rm f}}\frac{\partial\langle P^{(\chi)}_{\rm f}\rangle}{\partial x_{i}}\,\mathrm{d}t-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\langle\epsilon^{(\chi)}_{\rm f}\rangle}{k^{(\chi)}_{\rm f}}\left[U^{(\chi)}_{{\rm f},i}-\langle U^{(\chi)}_{{\rm f},i}\rangle\right]\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\phantom{=}-\frac{1}{\rho_{\rm f}}\frac{\partial\langle\widetilde{P}^{(\chi)}_{\rm f}\rangle}{\partial x_{i}}\,\mathrm{d}t+\widetilde{G}^{(\chi)}_{ij}\left[U^{(\chi)}_{{\rm f},j}-\langle U^{(\chi)}_{{\rm f},j}\rangle\right]\,\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{C_{0}\langle\widetilde{\epsilon}^{(\chi)}_{\rm f}\rangle}\,\mathrm{d}W_{i}~.

(259b)

The rationale behind the decomposition used on the rhs of Eq. (
259b
) is to introduce a distinction between two potentially different timescales: on the one hand, the timescale governing the rate at which turbulent kinetic energy is dissipated, which is represented by the term
k^{(\chi)}_{\rm f}/\langle\epsilon^{(\chi)}_{\rm f}\rangle
on the first line on the rhs of Eq. (
259b
); and, on the other hand, the timescale scaling as
k^{(\chi)}_{\rm f}/\langle\widetilde{\epsilon}^{(\chi)}_{\rm f}\rangle
with which turbulent kinetic energy is being exchanged, with neither production nor dissipation, between the velocity components, which means that on the second line on the rhs of Eq. (
259b
)
(\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}^{(\chi)})^{-1}
scales as
k^{(\chi)}_{\rm f}/\langle\widetilde{\epsilon}^{(\chi)}_{\rm f}\rangle
. Since both timescales are expressed as functions of
k_{\rm f}/\langle\epsilon_{\rm f}\rangle
, this explains indeed that two different turbulent kinetic energy rates are introduced in Eq. (
259b
), corresponding to two different scaling with respect to the small parameter
\chi
, as detailed below. Remember that the turbulent kinetic energy dissipation rate
\langle\epsilon_{\rm f}\rangle
is external to the description based on
\mathbf{Z}_{\rm f}=(\mathbf{X}_{\rm f},\mathbf{U}_{\rm f})
and appears therefore as the proper quantity to monitor to introduce different scaling of the timescales as a function of the small parameter
\chi
.

First, when
\chi=1
we take the two timescales as equal (
\langle\epsilon_{\rm f}\rangle=\langle\epsilon^{(\chi)}_{\rm f}\rangle=\langle\widetilde{\epsilon}^{(\chi)}_{\rm f}\rangle
) and gather the two pressure gradients terms into one (
\langle P_{\rm f}\rangle=\langle P^{(\chi)}_{\rm f}\rangle+\langle\widetilde{P}^{(\chi)}_{\rm f}\rangle
) to retrieve the classical form of a generalized Langevin model. The Reynolds equation is then

\frac{\partial\langle U_{{\rm f},i}\rangle}{\partial t}+\langle U_{{\rm f},j}\rangle\frac{\partial\langle U_{{\rm f},i}\rangle}{\partial x_{j}}=-\frac{1}{\rho_{\rm f}}\frac{\partial\langle P_{\rm f}\rangle}{\partial x_{i}}-\frac{\partial\langle u_{{\rm f},i}u_{{\rm f},j}\rangle}{\partial x_{j}}~,

(260)

where the Reynolds stress tensor,
\langle u_{{\rm f},i}u_{{\rm f},j}\rangle
, is the solution of a transport equation

\frac{\partial\langle u_{{\rm f},i}u_{{\rm f},j}\rangle}{\partial t}+\langle U_{{\rm f},k}\rangle\frac{\partial\langle u_{{\rm f},i}u_{{\rm f},j}\rangle}{\partial x_{k}}+\frac{\partial\langle u_{{\rm f},i}u_{{\rm f},j}u_{{\rm f},k}\rangle}{\partial x_{k}}=-\langle u_{{\rm f},i}u_{{\rm f},k}\rangle\frac{\partial\langle U_{{\rm f},j}\rangle}{\partial x_{k}}-\langle u_{{\rm f},j}u_{{\rm f},k}\rangle\frac{\partial\langle U_{{\rm f},i}\rangle}{\partial x_{k}}\\
+G_{ik}\langle u_{{\rm f},k}u_{{\rm f},j}\rangle+G_{jk}\langle u_{{\rm f},k}u_{{\rm f},i}\rangle+C_{0}\langle\epsilon_{\rm f}\rangle\delta_{ij}~,

(261)

where the return-to-equilibrium terms of Eq. (
259b
) have been added, i.e., with
\mathbf{G}=-1/2(\langle\epsilon_{\rm f}\rangle/k_{\rm f})\bm{\delta}+\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}
. For our present discussion, the important point is that the Reynolds stress tensor is a non-local quantity and is influenced by previous upstream values.

We now introduce the specific scaling in terms of a small parameter
\chi
whose limit to zero is to be taken and make full use of the difference between the two timescales.

If we write
k_{\rm f}^{(1)}
and
\langle\epsilon_{\rm f}^{(1)}\rangle
to refer to the level of turbulent kinetic energy and dissipation rates, respectively, coming from the ‘regular’ solution, for instance the one obtained with
\chi=1
and equipped with its corresponding modeled equation for
\langle\epsilon_{\rm f}^{(1)}\rangle
, we then choose
\langle\widetilde{\epsilon}^{(\chi)}_{\rm f}\rangle
to scale as
1/\chi^{2}
(or, more precisely, we choose
\langle\widetilde{\epsilon}^{(\chi)}_{\rm f}\rangle
so that
\langle\widetilde{\epsilon}^{(\chi)}_{\rm f}\rangle=1/\chi^{2}\,\langle\widetilde{\epsilon}_{\rm f}\rangle
with
\langle\widetilde{\epsilon}_{\rm f}\rangle\sim\langle\epsilon_{\rm f}^{(1)}\rangle
). Given that
\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}^{(\chi)}
scales as
\langle\widetilde{\epsilon}^{(\chi)}_{\rm f}\rangle/k^{(\chi)}_{\rm f}
and that we already know from the results of the fast-variable techniques recalled earlier that we can expect that
k^{(\chi)}_{\rm f}\sim 1/\chi
(or, more precisely, that
k^{(\chi)}_{\rm f}\simeq 1/\chi\,k_{\rm f}^{(1)}
), then we have
\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}^{(\chi)}=\langle\widetilde{\epsilon}^{(\chi)}_{\rm f}\rangle/k^{(\chi)}_{\rm f}\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}\sim 1/\chi\,\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}
where
\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}\sim\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}^{(1)}
. As shown earlier, we can also consider that the mean pressure gradient due to
\langle\widetilde{P}^{(\chi)}_{\rm f}\rangle
scales as
1/\chi
, as will be confirmed below, and can therefore be written as
\langle\widetilde{P}^{(\chi)}_{\rm f}\rangle\sim 1/\chi\langle\widetilde{P}_{\rm f}\rangle
.

On the other hand, for the timescale at which turbulent kinetic energy is dissipated, we choose
\langle\epsilon^{(\chi)}_{\rm f}\rangle
to scale on par with
k^{(\chi)}_{\rm f}
, that is as
1/\chi
(i.e., we take
\langle\epsilon^{(\chi)}_{\rm f}\rangle\sim 1/\chi\,\langle\epsilon_{\rm f}^{(1)}\rangle
). This is in line with the notion that
\langle\epsilon^{(\chi)}_{\rm f}\rangle
represents the ‘true’ dissipation rate, as can be seen on the rhs of Eq. (
261
) which indicates that
\langle\epsilon^{(\chi)}_{\rm f}\rangle
varies indeed as the production terms,
\langle u_{{\rm f},i}u_{{\rm f},k}\rangle\partial\langle U_{{\rm f},j}\rangle/\partial x_{k}
, and thus as
1/\chi
since the mean velocity field and its gradients remain of order 1 (note that, at this level, the correspondence with Eq. (
261
) is indicative and not a strict one since, due to the resulting local approximation as a Gaussian variable, the triple correlation vanishes). This implies that the timescale of turbulent kinetic energy dissipation,
k^{(\chi)}_{\rm f}/\langle\epsilon^{(\chi)}_{\rm f}\rangle
on the first line on the rhs of Eq. (
259b
), is of order 1 with respect to the small parameter
\chi
, i.e.,
k^{(\chi)}_{\rm f}/\langle\epsilon^{(\chi)}_{\rm f}\rangle\sim k^{(1)}_{\rm f}/\langle\epsilon^{(1)}_{\rm f}\rangle
. Accordingly, we can take that the pressure gradient due to
\langle P^{(\chi)}_{\rm f}\rangle
scales as
1
, i.e.,
\langle P^{(\chi)}_{\rm f}\rangle=\langle P_{\rm f}\rangle
with
\langle P_{\rm f}\rangle\sim\langle P^{(1)}_{\rm f}\rangle
.

Using these scaling relations, we can rewrite Eqs. (
259
) as

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}X^{(\chi)}_{{\rm f},i}

\displaystyle=U^{(\chi)}_{{\rm f},i}\,\mathrm{d}t~,

(262a)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}U^{(\chi)}_{{\rm f},i}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\rho_{\rm f}}\frac{\partial\langle P_{\rm f}\rangle}{\partial x_{i}}\,\mathrm{d}t-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\langle\epsilon^{(1}_{\rm f}\rangle}{k^{(1)}_{\rm f}}\left[U^{(\chi)}_{{\rm f},i}-\langle U^{(\chi)}_{{\rm f},i}\rangle\right]\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\phantom{=}-\frac{1}{\chi}\frac{1}{\rho_{\rm f}}\frac{\partial\langle\widetilde{P}_{\rm f}\rangle}{\partial x_{i}}\,\mathrm{d}t+\frac{1}{\chi}\widetilde{G}_{ij}\left[U^{(\chi)}_{{\rm f},j}-\langle U^{(\chi)}_{{\rm f},j}\rangle\right]\,\mathrm{d}t+\frac{1}{\chi}\sqrt{C_{0}\langle\widetilde{\epsilon}_{\rm f}\rangle}\,\mathrm{d}W_{i}~,

(262b)

and apply the fast-variable elimination techniques described above when
\chi\to 0
. The return-to-equilibrium term on the first line on the rhs of Eq. (
262b
) has been written as
\langle\epsilon^{(1}_{\rm f}\rangle/k^{(1)}_{\rm f}
and we have left out a possible constant appearing in front of this expression. This is not a concern since the terms on the first line on the rhs of Eq. (
262b
) play no role in the elimination process which is entirely governed by the terms scaling as
1/\chi
, that is by the terms on the second line on the rhs of Eq. (
262b
).

The equation satisfied by the mean velocity field
\langle\mathbf{U}^{(\chi)}_{\rm f}\rangle
(the mean Navier-Stokes equation) is

\frac{\partial\langle U^{(\chi)}_{{\rm f},i}\rangle}{\partial t}+\langle U^{(\chi)}_{{\rm f},j}\rangle\frac{\partial\langle U^{(\chi)}_{{\rm f},i}\rangle}{\partial x_{j}}=-\frac{1}{\rho_{\rm f}}\frac{\partial\langle P_{\rm f}\rangle}{\partial x_{i}}-\frac{1}{\chi}\left\{\frac{1}{\rho_{\rm f}}\frac{\partial\langle\widetilde{P}_{\rm f}\rangle}{\partial x_{i}}+\frac{\partial\left[\chi\langle u^{(\chi)}_{{\rm f},i}u^{(\chi)}_{{\rm f},j}\rangle\right]}{\partial x_{j}}\right\}~,

(263)

where the equivalent of the Reynolds stress tensor,
A_{ij}=\chi\langle u^{(\chi)}_{{\rm f},i}u^{(\chi)}_{{\rm f},j}\rangle
is now a local term which, in the limit
\chi\to 0
, is the solution of the matrix equation

\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}\mathbf{A}+\mathbf{A}\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}^{\bot}=-C_{0}\langle\widetilde{\epsilon}_{\rm f}\rangle\bm{\delta}~.

(264)

It is instructive to work out the resulting form of Eq. (
263
) corresponding to two different closures of
\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}
.

First, if we retain the SLM, then the decomposition used in Eq. (
259b
) indicates that

\widetilde{G}^{(\chi)}_{ij}=-\frac{3\,C_{0}}{4}\frac{\langle\widetilde{\epsilon}^{(\chi)}_{\rm f}\rangle}{k^{(\chi)}_{\rm f}}\delta_{ij}\Longrightarrow\widetilde{G}_{ij}\simeq-\frac{3\,C_{0}}{4}\frac{\langle\widetilde{\epsilon}_{\rm f}\rangle}{k^{(1)}_{\rm f}}\delta_{ij}\Longrightarrow A_{ij}=\frac{2}{3}k^{(1)}_{\rm f}\delta_{ij}~.

(265)

Then by taking
\widetilde{P}_{\rm f}
in Eq. (
263
) so that
\widetilde{P}_{\rm f}+2/3k^{(1)}_{\rm f}
is constant, we get that the equation satisfied by
\langle\mathbf{U}_{\rm f}\rangle
obtained as the limit of the one satisfied by
\langle U^{(\chi)}_{\rm f}\rangle
when
\chi\to 0
is

\frac{\partial\langle U_{{\rm f},i}\rangle}{\partial t}+\langle U_{{\rm f},j}\rangle\frac{\partial\langle U_{{\rm f},i}\rangle}{\partial x_{j}}=-\frac{1}{\rho_{\rm f}}\frac{\partial\langle P_{\rm f}\rangle}{\partial x_{i}}~.

(266)

This is the equivalent of the Euler equations obtained as the first-order approximation in the derivation of the equations for hydrodynamics.

Second, if we consider a more elaborate closure of the matrix
\mathbf{G}
in GLMs, such as the IP (Isotropization of Production) model
(
Pope 2000
, chapter 12)
, we have

\displaystyle\widetilde{G}^{(\chi)}_{ij}

\displaystyle=\left(-\frac{3\,C_{0}}{4}-C_{2}\frac{\mathcal{P}}{\langle\widetilde{\epsilon}^{(\chi)}_{\rm f}\rangle}\right)\frac{\langle\widetilde{\epsilon}^{(\chi)}_{\rm f}\rangle}{k^{(\chi)}_{\rm f}}\delta_{ij}+C_{2}\frac{\partial\langle U_{{\rm f},i}\rangle}{\partial x_{j}}

(267)

\displaystyle=\frac{\langle\widetilde{\epsilon}^{(\chi)}_{\rm f}\rangle}{k^{(\chi)}_{\rm f}}\left[\left(-\frac{3\,C_{0}}{4}-C_{2}\frac{\mathcal{P}^{(\chi)}}{\langle\widetilde{\epsilon}^{(\chi)}_{\rm f}\rangle}\right)\delta_{ij}+C_{2}\frac{k^{(\chi)}_{\rm f}}{\langle\widetilde{\epsilon}^{(\chi)}_{\rm f}\rangle}\frac{\partial\langle U_{{\rm f},i}\rangle}{\partial x_{j}}\right]~,

(268)

where
\mathcal{P}^{(\chi)}
is the turbulent kinetic energy production term (varying as
1/\chi
). With the various scaling given above, this gives

\widetilde{G}_{ij}\simeq-\frac{3\,C_{0}}{4}\frac{\langle\widetilde{\epsilon}_{\rm f}\rangle}{k^{(1)}_{\rm f}}\left[\delta_{ij}+\chi\,\frac{4C_{2}}{3C_{0}}\left(\frac{\mathcal{P}^{(1)}}{\langle\widetilde{\epsilon}_{\rm f}\rangle}\delta_{ij}-\frac{k^{(1)}_{\rm f}}{\langle\widetilde{\epsilon}_{\rm f}\rangle}\frac{\partial\langle U_{{\rm f},i}\rangle}{\partial x_{j}}\right)\right]~.

(269)

We can then make a first-order approximation of the matrix
\widetilde{G}^{-1}
in terms of the small parameter
\chi
to obtain the first-order approximation of
\mathbf{A}
from Eq. (
264
). Given that
A_{ij}
is a symmetrical tensor and leaving out the exact calculation of the constants appearing in front of the terms of the series, we obtain

A_{ij}\simeq\frac{2}{3}k^{(1)}_{\rm f}\delta_{ij}+\chi\,C_{\rm A,1}\frac{k^{(1)}_{\rm f}}{\langle\widetilde{\epsilon}_{\rm f}\rangle}\mathcal{P}^{(1)}\delta_{ij}-\chi\,C_{\rm A,2}\frac{(k^{(1)}_{\rm f})^{2}}{\langle\widetilde{\epsilon}_{\rm f}\rangle}S_{ij}~,

(270)

where
S_{ij}
is the strain rate tensor, i.e.,
S_{ij}=1/2\left(\partial\langle U_{{\rm f},i}\rangle/\partial x_{j}+\partial\langle U_{{\rm f},j}\rangle/\partial x_{i}\right)
. By making the same evaluation of
\widetilde{P}_{\rm f}
as in the zero-order above (the Euler equation) and by using that
\langle u^{(\chi)}_{{\rm f},i}u^{(\chi)}_{{\rm f},j}\rangle=A_{ij}/\chi
, we obtain from Eq. (
263
) that the equation satisfied by
\langle\mathbf{U}_{\rm f}\rangle
as the limit of the one for
\langle\mathbf{U}^{(\chi)}_{\rm f}\rangle
when
\chi\to 0
is now

\frac{\partial\langle U_{{\rm f},i}\rangle}{\partial t}+\langle U_{{\rm f},j}\rangle\frac{\partial\langle U_{{\rm f},i}\rangle}{\partial x_{j}}=-\frac{1}{\rho_{\rm f}}\frac{\partial\langle P_{\rm f}\rangle}{\partial x_{i}}+\nu_{\rm ft}\left(\frac{\partial\langle U_{{\rm f},i}\rangle}{\partial x_{j}}+\frac{\partial\langle U_{{\rm f},j}\rangle}{\partial x_{i}}\right)~,

(271)

where the term involving
C_{\rm A,1}
has been added to the pressure and where
\nu_{\rm ft}
is referred to as the turbulent viscosity. It is seen that
\nu_{\rm ft}
is expressed by
\nu_{\rm ft}=C_{\rm ft}\,k_{\rm f}^{2}/\langle\epsilon_{\rm f}\rangle
with
C_{\rm ft}
a constant, which is a consistent outcome since, with the present scaling of
k_{\rm f}
(as
1/\chi
) and of
\langle\epsilon_{\rm f}\rangle
(as
1/\chi^{2}
), it is indeed the ratio
k_{\rm f}^{2}/\langle\epsilon_{\rm f}\rangle
that remains of order 1. Therefore, the first-order approximation in terms of the small parameter
\chi
leads to an equivalent form as the hydrodynamics equation where transport coefficients, such as molecular viscosity, appear in the first-order approximation of the Chapman-Enskog iterative approach. In that sense, the length
k_{\rm f}^{3/2}/\langle\epsilon_{\rm f}\rangle
appears as the equivalent of the ‘mean free path’ but this is a mere analogy since the physical picture as free flight and collisions does not apply and is more the characteristic length over which velocities remain significantly correlated. The important point in our context is that we have now a local closure of the Reynolds stress tensor which is consistent with models based on the turbulent-viscosity concept.

These results are technical in nature and are meant to describe the steps towards the correct diffusive regime as the limit of over-damped Langevin equations for particle velocities. They demonstrate that consistent results are also obtained for velocity statistics (i.e., the mean velocity field) by manipulating carefully the white-noise limit while still handling SDEs. Yet, they should not necessarily be regarded as providing physical arguments for the concept of a turbulent viscosity or diffusivity. Given the length at which we have been obliged to go, it can even be said that they point to their lack of universal validity. There is indeed a notable difference with classical kinetic theory. When going from molecular motions to the Navier-Stokes equations, the ratio of the molecular relaxation timescale to a characteristic fluid one
\mathcal{L}/\delta U
, where
\delta U
is a typical velocity difference over
\mathcal{L}
the size of the domain considered, is indeed very small and of the order of
Kn\times Ma
, where
Kn
and
Ma
are the Knudsen and Mach numbers, respectively. The Newton law is therefore an excellent approximation. Except in some special situations, this is not so in turbulence since the typical timescale ratio,
k_{\rm f}/\langle\epsilon_{\rm f}\rangle\partial\langle U_{\rm f}\rangle/\partial x
, is of the order of
1-5

Pope 2000
. This is a reminder of the importance of carefully sorting out slow and fast variables and of the selection of the particle state vector. In turbulence modeling, it is physically more justified to retain particle velocities.

V.3
Summary and two-way influences

The recognition of the importance of keeping track of the structural variables attached to the solute is characteristic of several complex fluid models, while other models introduce an order parameter to capture long-range order
Öttinger 2005
. This is exemplified by the convected Maxwell model for the pressure tensor in polymeric fluids, which is recovered from a kinetic theory for Hookean dumbbells
Venerus and Öttinger 2018
. In that sense, turbulent dispersed two-phase flow modeling follows in the same footsteps with the reference particle state vector
\mathbf{Z}_{\rm p}=(\mathbf{X}_{\rm p},\mathbf{U}_{\rm p},\mathbf{U}_{\rm s})
whose variables are treated as stochastic processes.

We have seen that the introduction of Brownian effects is as straightforward as for complex fluids. Accounting for turbulent fluctuations in non-fully-resolved flows is, however, far more challenging.

Another characteristic of complex fluid models is that they are formulated in the diffusive limit by neglecting particle inertia. In that respect, recent studies of the over-damped Langevin limit have brought more rigorous formulations which shed new light into this question. For example, it is important to notice that the correct diffusive terms appear only when the fast variable to be eliminated depends on the slow one where the white-noise terms emerge (in other words, when
\mathbf{U}_{\rm f}
is the first-order derivative of
\mathbf{X}_{\rm f}
). However, in turbulent two-phase flow modeling
\mathbf{U}_{\rm s}
is (loosely speaking) the second-order derivative of
\mathbf{X}_{\rm p}
. When considering the diffusive limit when both
\tau_{\rm p}
and
T_{\rm L}^{*}
become very small, this leads to the surprising result that
\lim_{\tau_{\rm p}\to 0}\lim_{T_{\rm L}^{*}\to 0}\neq\lim_{T_{\rm L}^{*}\to 0}\lim_{\tau_{\rm p}\to 0}
(as illustrated on Fig.
26
).

Figure 26
:
Sketch of the key issue when formulating the diffusive limit of a Langevin model.

To manifest this effect, we write the general model for
\mathbf{Z}_{\rm p}
as

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}X_{{\rm p},i}

\displaystyle=U_{{\rm p},i}\,\mathrm{d}t~,

(272a)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}U_{{\rm p},i}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\tau_{\rm p}}\left(U_{{\rm s},i}-U_{{\rm p},i}\right)\mathrm{d}t~,

(272b)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}U_{{\rm s},i}

\displaystyle=G_{ij}^{*}(t,\mathbf{X}_{\rm p}(t))\left[U_{{\rm s},j}-\phi_{j}^{*}(t,\mathbf{X}_{\rm p}(t))\right]\mathrm{d}t+\sigma_{ij}^{*}(t,\mathbf{X}_{\rm p}(t))\,\mathrm{d}W_{j}~,

(272c)

where the dependencies of
\mathbf{G}^{*}
,
\bm{\phi}^{*}
and
\bm{\sigma}^{*}
in Eq. (
272c
) are indicated to bring out that they depend on
\mathbf{X}_{\rm p}(t)
which is typically their twice-integrated variable. In Eq. (
272c
), the field
\bm{\phi}^{*}
can be written as

\phi_{i}^{*}(t,\mathbf{x})=\langle U_{{\rm f},i}\rangle(t,\mathbf{x})+\left(G_{ij}^{*}\right)^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{\rho_{\rm f}}\frac{\partial\langle P_{\rm f}\rangle}{\partial x_{j}}+\Pi_{j}\right)(t,\mathbf{x})~,

(273)

where
\bm{\Pi}
represents the additional mean terms accounting for the crossing-trajectory effect, while it is sufficient to consider that
\mathbf{G}^{*}\simeq-1/T_{\rm L}^{*}\,\bm{\delta}
(without considering explicitly the different Csanady timescales) and
\sigma_{ij}^{*}\sim\sqrt{\langle\epsilon_{\rm f}\rangle}
for the sake of simplicity and without any impact on the following outcomes. When we first take the limit
\tau_{\rm p}\to 0
, the two-phase flow model in Eqs. (
272
) reverts by construction to the fluid one, cf. Eqs. (
230
), and the same result obtained in Eq. (
237
), or in Eq. (
239
), is retrieved when we take the second limit
T_{\rm L}\to 0
(since
T_{\rm L}^{*}(\tau_{\rm p}=0)=T_{\rm L}
). However, if we first take the limit
T_{\rm L}^{*}\to 0
, the detailed stochastic calculus
(
Minier 2016
, section 9.3.3)
shows that the limit system when
\mathbf{U}_{\rm f}
is eliminated is

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}X_{{\rm p},i}^{(T_{\rm L}^{*})}

\displaystyle=U_{{\rm p},i}^{(T_{\rm L}^{*})}\,\mathrm{d}t~,

(274a)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}U_{{\rm p},i}^{(T_{\rm L}^{*})}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\tau_{\rm p}}\,U_{{\rm p},i}^{(T_{\rm L}^{*})}\mathrm{d}t+\frac{1}{\tau_{\rm p}}\,\phi_{i}^{*}(t,\mathbf{X}^{(T_{\rm L}^{*})}_{\rm p}(t))\mathrm{d}t+\frac{1}{\tau_{\rm p}}\,\left((G^{*})^{-1}\sigma^{*}\right)_{ij}(t,\mathbf{X}^{(T_{\rm L}^{*})}_{\rm p}(t))\,\mathrm{d}W_{j}~,

(274b)

where the notations
\mathbf{X}_{\rm p}^{(T_{\rm L}^{*})}
and
\mathbf{U}_{\rm p}^{(T_{\rm L}^{*})}
indicate that the limit
T_{\rm L}^{*}\to 0
is taken. Note that the rhs of Eq. (
274b
) corresponds to what the naive formulation (i.e., writing directly
\mathrm{d}\mathbf{U}_{\rm s}=0
in Eq. (
272c
)) would yield. Note also that the field
\bm{\phi}^{*}
remains regular in that limit case since the mean velocity
\langle\mathbf{U}_{\rm f}\rangle
remains so, as demonstrated above. By taking the second limit
\tau_{\rm p}\to 0
, no extra term appears from this second stochastic calculus and we obtain that the predicted diffusive limit is

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}X_{{\rm p},i}^{(\tau_{\rm p},T_{\rm L}^{*})}

\displaystyle=\Phi_{i}\,\mathrm{d}t+\left(G^{-1}\sigma\right)_{ij}\mathrm{d}W_{i}~,

(275)

\displaystyle=\langle U_{{\rm f},i}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+(G_{ij})^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{\rho_{\rm f}}\frac{\partial\langle P_{\rm f}\rangle}{\partial x_{j}}\right)\mathrm{d}t+\left(G^{-1}\sigma^{*}\right)_{ij}\mathrm{d}W_{j}

(276)

where the second term on the rhs of Eq. (
232
) is missing. In the simple case of isotropic tensors, we have

\mathrm{d}X_{{\rm p},i}^{(\tau_{\rm p},T_{\rm L}^{*})}=\langle U_{{\rm f},i}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+T_{\rm L}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}}\left[\frac{1}{2}T_{\rm L}\,\sigma^{2}\right]\,\mathrm{d}t+\left(T_{\rm L}\,\sigma\right)\,\mathrm{d}W_{i}~,

(277)

which is clearly wrong by comparison with the correct form in Eq. (
240
). Actually, the non-zero value of the particle relaxation timescale
\tau_{\rm p}
filters out some of the fluctuations of
\mathbf{U}_{\rm s}
, when the white-noise limit is considered for the fluid velocity seen, which cannot be retrieved afterward. This leads to a truncated, and ultimately wrong, limit expression. The diffusive limit should therefore be addressed with care when several timescales are involved and it is believed that this is an area where progress in two-phase flow modeling can be of interest for complex fluids.

VI
Statistical modeling of particle collisions in turbulent flows

Open any textbook on non-equilibrium statistical mechanics and you will find the Boltzmann equation as the archetypal description of particle-particle interactions. The Boltzmann equation is undoubtedly a major achievement and a well-established framework for short-range particle-particle interactions, especially for rarefied gases
Reif 1985
;
Liboff 1998
;
Balescu 1997
;
Keizer 2012
. It is therefore justified to refer to it when considering particle collisions in another context. The specific assumptions underlying the classical form of the Boltzmann equation are, however, often taken for granted. This is a regrettable oversight since collisions of particles transported by random media can challenge some aspects of the Boltzmann picture. With a view toward modeling particle collisions in turbulent flows, the purpose of this section is to revisit the key characteristics of the Boltzmann equation to bring out the limitations that need to be addressed.

Since there are several detailed accounts of the Boltzmann theory, we limit ourselves to recalling the main points of interest for the present discussion. In its final formulation, the Boltzmann approach is a statistical description developed in terms of the single-particle distribution function
f(t;\mathbf{y}_{\rm p},\mathbf{V}_{\rm p},d_{\rm p})
in which only the kinetic variables
(\mathbf{X}_{\rm p},\mathbf{U}_{\rm p})
are retained as well as the particle diameter
d_{\rm p}
(or its volume
\mathcal{V}_{\rm p}
). The physical picture consists in a process where particles undergo free flights and binary collisions. Note that the Boltzmann equation can easily account for the effects of an external and conservative force field (thus, deriving from a potential) so that particles do not necessarily follow straight lines in-between collisions but this does not change our discussion and we can still refer to free flights. In the Boltzmann-Grad limit where particle diameters are very small but with finite cross-section (i.e., in the limit when the number of particles
N_{\rm p}\to+\infty
and
d_{\rm p}\to 0
, but with
N_{\rm p}\,d_{\rm p}^{2}
remaining constant), collisions are regarded as instantaneous and point-wise elastic interactions so that mass, momentum and kinetic energy are conserved during collisions which appear as random redistributive events. When a collision occurs between a particle with a state vector
(\mathbf{X}_{\rm p},\mathbf{U}_{\rm p},d_{\rm p})
and a collisional partner whose variables are noted
(\mathbf{X}_{\rm c,p},\mathbf{U}_{\rm c,p},d_{\rm c,p})
, the transformation rules expressing the post-collisional velocities
\mathbf{U}^{\rm ac}_{\rm p}
and
\mathbf{U}^{\rm ac}_{\rm c,p}
in terms of the pre-collisional ones
\mathbf{U}_{\rm p}
and
\mathbf{U}_{\rm c,p}
are

\displaystyle\mathbf{U}^{\rm ac}_{\rm p}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{U}_{\rm p}-\frac{2m_{\rm c,p}}{m_{\rm p}+m_{\rm c,p}}\left(\mathbf{U}_{\rm p}-\mathbf{U}_{\rm c,p},\mathbf{e}\right)\cdot\mathbf{e}~,

(278a)

\displaystyle\mathbf{U}^{\rm ac}_{\rm c,p}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{U}_{\rm c,p}+\frac{2m_{\rm p}}{m_{\rm p}+m_{\rm c,p}}\left(\mathbf{U}_{\rm p}-\mathbf{U}_{\rm c,p},\mathbf{e}\right)\cdot\mathbf{e}~,

(278b)

with
(\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b})
the scalar product between two vectors
\mathbf{a}
and
\mathbf{b}
in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
,
\mathbf{e}
a unit vector on the unit sphere
\mathcal{S}^{2}
representing the orientation of the collision event, and
m_{\rm p}=\rho_{\rm p}\pi d^{3}_{\rm p}/6
(resp.
m_{\rm c,p}=\rho_{\rm p}\pi d^{3}_{\rm c,p}/6
) the mass of the target particle (resp. its collisional partner). These relations are easily inverted to provide the pre-collisional velocities
\mathbf{U}^{\rm bc}_{\rm p}
and
\mathbf{U}^{\rm bc}_{\rm c,p}
yielding
\mathbf{U}_{\rm p}
and
\mathbf{U}_{\rm c,p}
as the outcome of the collisional event, which we write as
\mathbf{U}^{\rm bc}_{\rm p}=\mathbf{U}^{\rm bc}_{\rm p}(\mathbf{U}_{\rm p},\mathbf{U}_{\rm c,p},\mathbf{e})
and
\mathbf{U}^{\rm bc}_{\rm c,p}=\mathbf{U}^{\rm bc}_{\rm c,p}(\mathbf{U}_{\rm p},\mathbf{U}_{\rm c,p},\mathbf{e})
. With these notations and leaving out external force fields, the Boltzmann equation is expressed in sample space for the single-particle distribution function
f(t;\mathbf{y}_{\rm p},\mathbf{V}_{\rm p},\widehat{d}_{\rm p})
as

\frac{\partial f}{\partial t}+V_{{\rm p},k}\frac{\partial f}{\partial y_{{\rm p},k}}=\frac{\partial\phi_{\rm ext}(\mathbf{y})}{\partial y_{k}}\frac{\partial f}{\partial V_{k}}+Q_{\rm coll}(f)~,

(279)

where
\widehat{d}_{\rm p}
is the sample space variable for
d_{\rm p}
and
-\partial\phi_{\rm ext}(\mathbf{x})/\partial x_{k}
an external force field (per unit mass) which is left out from now on for the sake of simplicity. In Eq. (
279
),
Q_{\rm coll}(f)
is a gain and loss term accounting for the collisional events in Eqs. (
278
) over all possible collisional partners which gives
